| M50 | §11.3.1.1(1), (11.3.1.3)–(11.3.1.4), p. 494, also (1.8.3.9), p. 51 | After invoking “(lifts of the) Schauder basis elements on the subquotients (11.3.1.3)”, the author writes “We are going to omit all of this from this overview.” The global stage needs a specified Fourier-support level and lifts realizing its basis with estimates in the half-twisted strip norm. Compactness of $C_F/\pi_v^{\mathbf Z}$ alone supplies no such estimates. Theorem 7.3.3.4 on p. 360 treats $S'$-local Paley–Wiener quotients. Page 496 chooses a level for the global basis elements and invokes the expansion (11.2.2.12) to pass to finite-rank kernels. That expansion on p. 491 treats the single-prime quotient at $a=0$. The estimate that transfers it to the half-twisted strip is missing. In Lean, `PrimaryDivisorialThetaSubquotient` defines the algebraic quotient of consecutive stages. | #4506, #5352. For the related failure of a uniform global strip bound as the number of depleted primes grows, see *Further repairs, attempted closures, and the remaining estimate*, §§2.5–2.6, pp. 12–15 (outside index 30). | reviewed |

## Reported from reading, beyond what has been formalized

The scouts who surveyed existing Lean, and outside readers, also read ahead in the paper. These
reports concern parts that are not stated in Lean yet, so nothing here has been tested by the
formalization. "Reported" means that nobody in the project has checked it.

| # | Where | Report | Source | Checked |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| R1 | Proposition 8.2.0.4(1),(2), p. 386 | Uniqueness modulo the embedded local unit group is reported to fail for the full idele group as printed: for the adele that is zero at a finite place v and one elsewhere, both the identity and the idele with a uniformizer at v fix it. The proof itself computes the stabilizer in the full group as the whole local multiplicative group. Restricting to norm one is a candidate correction; Theorem 8.3.0.1(1) cites the proposition. | [`survey/geometry.md`](RiemannMasullo/External/survey/geometry.md); also reported by Goyal, Naslund | reviewed |
| R2 | §1.4.3, p. 29, and §1.8.4.3, p. 54 | The bound on the archimedean terms, claimed to be uniform in the cutoff and used for a Montel extraction, is reported to fail for the printed family of test functions, already for ζ: with nonnegative approximants tending to t⁻² on t > 1, Fatou's lemma makes the archimedean contribution diverge as the cutoff neighbourhood of 1 shrinks. The report says this concerns the passage to the limit in the introduction's sketch and leaves the trace identity itself undecided. A sign discrepancy in the prime sum on p. 29 is also reported. | [`survey/endgame.md`](RiemannMasullo/External/survey/endgame.md); also reported by Goyal, Naslund | reviewed |
| R3 | Chapter 11 and §12.4–12.5, pp. 475–520 (Theorem 12.5.1.1) | Checked item by item on 22 November against the pages and the Lean statements. What held is now in the entries that name issue #2469. | issue #2469, pull request #2734; also reported by Goyal, Naslund | moved |
| R4 | Theorem 12.5.1.1, pp. 519–520, and Theorem B.4.4.7, pp. 623–625 | The two sides differ by the factor μ̄₁(C_F¹): the right side integrates against the Tamagawa measure μ̄, for which μ̄₁(C_F¹) = 2^{r₁}(2π)^{r₂}h_F R_F/(w_F \|d_F\|^{1/2}) by (A.3.2.1), p. 560, while the left uses the canonical local measures, and the proof quotes Theorem 12.4.1.1 without its factor 1/μ̄_{S,1}(J¹_{F,S}). The factor is π/4 for ℚ(i), π/(3√3) for ℚ(√−3), and 1 for ℚ. With the factor divided out, and a strip that contains all the zeros, the identity is the explicit formula of Theorem B.4.4.7, which carries 1/μ̄₁(C_F¹) on each global term. The interval I is not introduced in the statement; the proof takes I = [−α, α]. For the degenerate strip α = 0 the interior of the strip is empty and so is the sum over the zeros, and the identity then fails already for ℚ: for a test function h(\|g\|) with h supported near 2 the place 2 contributes (log 2/√2)·h(2) to the left side, while every other term is an integral of h against a bounded density. | issue #587; also reported by Goyal, Naslund, issue #2469 | reviewed |
| R5 | Theorem 10.3.1.1, (10.3.1.18), p. 451 | Moved to F214: this part of the paper has since been stated in Lean, and the report was checked against the page. | issue #1169 | moved |
| R6 | Theorem 11.1.2.1(2), (11.1.2.6), p. 480 | Moved to F215: this part of the paper has since been stated in Lean, and the report was checked against the page. | issue #1170 | moved |
| R7 | Lemma 5.3.1.1, p. 240 | Moved to F213: this part of the paper has since been stated in Lean, and the report was checked against the page. | issue #1177 | moved |
| R8 | Proposition 12.2.2.2(4), pp. 508–512 | Moved to F216: this part of the paper has since been stated in Lean, and the report was checked against the page. | issue #2047 | moved |
| R9 | §12.4.1, Step II, pp. 516–517 | The norm cutoff m(\|x\| \|y\|_p) is used as a multiplier on the additive Schwartz–Bruhat space, but it does not preserve it: already for one real and one finite place, e^{−x²} 1_{ℤ_p}(y) times the cutoff has no common open period at p. | issue #2109; also reported by issue #2469 | reviewed |
| R10 | Proposition 3.4.2.1, proof, p. 145, citing Corollary B.7.2.5, p. 653 | The proof reduces the claim to convergence on the line $\sigma=1$ of “the countable net of meromorphic functions … defined by the partial Euler products $S'\mapsto\prod_{v\in S'}L_v(\chi_v\lvert\cdot\rvert_v^s)$ … as $S'$ varies over all finite sets of finite places of $F$ such that $S'\cap S=\varnothing$”, and says “This is indeed the case by Corollary B.7.2.5”. That corollary concerns the products over $N(\mathfrak p)<x$. On the line the product does not converge absolutely, so the net over all finite sets diverges. For the quadratic character modulo $5$ over $\mathbf Q$ at $s=1$ the factors are $(1-\chi(p)/p)^{-1}$, and $\sum_{p\equiv1\,(5)}1/p$ and $\sum_{p\equiv2\,(5)}1/p$ both diverge, so beyond any finite set the partial product can be multiplied by more than $2$ or by less than $1/2$. The statement itself fails by I96. | Goyal, *Audit of Masullo's proposed proof*, `dossiers/notes/appendices.md`, “Ordered Euler products do not define a Lebesgue integral at the 1-line” | reviewed |
| R11 | Thm. 8.1.0.1(1), (8.1.0.2), p. 382 (§8.1 starts on p. 381) | The paper says: “where $\mathcal S_p(\mathbf A_{F,S})\subset\mathcal S(\mathbf A_{F,S})$ is the closed subspace” in (8.1.0.2). The displayed tensor product uses $\mathcal S(F_v^\times)$ for $v\in S_f-S_p$. This fails: Over ℚ take S={∞,2,3}, p=2, χ=1, Pic(ℤ[1/6])=1, a nonzero real test flat at zero, 1_{ℤ₂}, and h(3ᵏu)=exp(−k²). The 3-adic factor fails local constancy at zero. | Naslund, *A proof review of The Music of the Primes*, `review/page-index.tsv` at 381 (§8.1); `review/proof-audit.tex` 303 | reviewed |
| R12 | Lemma 8.2.0.3(2), quotient topology, p. 385 | The paper says: The subspace topology on $D_F^1$ inherited from $\mathbf A_F$ agrees with the quotient topology from $\mathrm{GL}_1(\mathbf A_F)^1\times Z_F$. This fails: For $F=\mathbf Q$ the orbit of $e_\infty=(0,1_f)$ consists of $(0,u)$ with $u$ any finite idele, since the real coordinate absorbs the norm. Replacing the $p$-component of $1_f$ by $p$ gives a sequence of points tending to $e_\infty$ in the additive adeles as $p\to\infty$. The set $U=\{0\}\times\widehat{\mathbf Z}^\times$ contains $e_\infty$ and excludes every point of the sequence. Its preimage is $\{g:g_f\in\widehat{\mathbf Z}^\times\}\times\{e_\infty\}$, which is open since $e_\infty$ is isolated in $Z_{\mathbf Q}$. Thus $U$ is quotient-open and fails to be subspace-open. | Goyal, *Audit of Masullo's proposed proof*, `report/report.md`, “Other established errors and their reach”, second item; `dossiers/notes/synthesis.md`, “Established 4” | reviewed |
| R13 | Lemma 8.2.0.3, sentence after part (2), p. 385, and local compactness argument, p. 386 | The paper says: The global space $D_F^1$ with its adelic subspace topology is locally compact. This fails: Take $F=\mathbf Q$ and $e_\infty=(0,1_f)$. In any basic adelic neighbourhood of $e_\infty$, choose distinct unconstrained primes $p_1,p_2,\ldots$. Set the components at $p_1,\ldots,p_n$ equal to $p_1,\ldots,p_n$ and keep all others as in $e_\infty$. Each point is in the orbit of $e_\infty$. Their adelic limit has infinitely many nonunit finite components, so it lies outside $D_{\mathbf Q}^1$. Any compact neighbourhood in $D_{\mathbf Q}^1$ would contain such a sequence and would be closed in the Hausdorff ambient adeles. | Goyal, *Audit of Masullo's proposed proof*, `report/report.md`, “Other established errors and their reach”, second item; `dossiers/notes/synthesis.md`, “Established 4” | reviewed |
| R14 | Lemma 8.2.0.3(2), proof, p. 386 (statement p. 385) | The proof calls the ordinary orbit and the single-zero orbits “a locally finite cover of $D_F^1$ by closed subsets.” For $F=\mathbf Q$ let $e_p$ have zero $p$-component and ones elsewhere. It belongs to the orbit $D_p^1$. A basic additive adelic neighbourhood of $1$ restricts only finitely many components and admits $0\in\mathbf Z_p$ at every other finite place. Hence $e_p\to1$ and every such neighbourhood meets infinitely many distinct orbits. | Naslund, *A proof review of The Music of the Primes*, `review/proof-audit.tex` 316; `review/page-index.tsv` at 384 | reviewed |
| R15 | Lemma 9.3.2.4, p. 425 | The paper says: For $\phi\in\mathcal S_{\mathrm E}(G)$ “the number of zeros of $M(\phi)(\chi\lvert\cdot\rvert_G^z)$ contained in the box $I\times i[T,T+1]$ is $O(\log\lvert T\rvert)$”. This fails: Take $G=\mathbf R_{>0}$, $\chi=1$ and $I=[-1,1]$. In the coordinate $x=\log t$, the Gaussian $g_c(x)=(4\pi c)^{-1/2}e^{-x^2/(4c)}$ has Mellin transform $e^{cz^2}$ for $\Re c>0$. The test $(g_{1+i}-g_{1-i})/(2i)$ has transform $e^{z^2}\sin(z^2)$. Its zeros at $i\sqrt{n\pi}$ give $(2T+1)/\pi+O(1)$ zeros in the box as $T\to+\infty$. The proof needs a lower bound for the transform's modulus at the moving centre in Jensen's inequality. | Goyal, *Audit of Masullo's proposed proof*, `paper/preprint.tex`, §6; `dossiers/notes/spectral.md`, §2; Naslund, *A proof review of The Music of the Primes*, `review/proof-audit.tex` 324; `gpt-pro-conversations/conversation-1.md` answer 1 §4; `gpt-pro-conversations/conversation-2.md` answer 1 §5.1 | reviewed |
| R16 | Lemma 9.3.2.5, pp. 425–426 | The paper says: If all the zeros of $M(\phi)(\chi\lvert\cdot\rvert_G^z)$ lie in the interior of $\mathbf C_I$, “the quotient of $\mathcal S_{\mathrm E}(G)$ by the convolution ideal $\phi*\mathcal S_{\mathrm E}(G)$ admits an absolute Schauder basis whose set of coefficient functionals is given by the point-evaluation functionals at the zeros of $M(\phi)(\chi\lvert\cdot\rvert_G^z)$ and their derivatives”. This fails: For $G=\mathbf R_{>0}$, $\chi=1$ and $I=[-1,1]$, take log-Gaussians with transforms $U(z)=e^{z^2}$ and $V(z)=e^{z^2/2}$. The first has no zeros, yet the second represents a nonzero quotient class. A convolution preimage would have transform $V/U=e^{-z^2/2}$, unbounded on $i\mathbf R$, where transforms of integrable tests are bounded. Even for the closed ideal there is a counterexample. The function $D(z)=e^{z^2}\prod_{n\ge1}(1+z^2/n^2)(1+z^2/(n+\delta_n)^2)$ with $\delta_n=e^{-n^3}$ is the transform of an element of $\mathcal S_{\mathrm E}$ (the product is at most $(\sinh(\pi\lvert z\rvert)/(\pi\lvert z\rvert))^2$), with simple zeros $\pm in$, $\pm i(n+\delta_n)$. The seminorm $p(B)=\sup_n\lvert B(in)-B(i(n+\delta_n))\rvert/\delta_n\le\sup_y\lvert B'(iy)\rvert$ is continuous and vanishes on the closed ideal. A vector dual to the evaluation at $in$ has $p\ge e^{n^3}$, so the terms of the expansion of $e^{z^2}$ have $p\ge e^{n^3-n^2}$ and do not tend to zero. | Goyal, *Audit of Masullo's proposed proof*, `paper/preprint.tex`, §7 (first part) and §3; `dossiers/notes/spectral.md`, §2; Naslund, *A proof review of The Music of the Primes*, `review/proof-audit.tex` 325; `gpt-pro-conversations/conversation-2.md` answer 1 §5.2 | reviewed |
| R17 | Proof of Theorem 11.3.2.1, displayed containment for $\Xi_k$, p. 495 | The paper says: After defining $\Xi_k$ by the condition of mapping to a uniformizer at some new place, the paper gives $\{\xi g\tau(t),\,t\in\mathbf R_{>0},\,g\in E_F^1,\,\xi\in\Xi_k\}\subset\mathrm{GL}_1(\mathbf A_{F,S'})\times\prod_{v\notin S'}\mathcal O_{F_v}^{\times}$ “for $S'$ large enough containing $S$ and $P_{k+1}-P_k$.” This fails: In the rational case of the theorem take the first shell at 2, $g=1$, $t=1$. For any such finite $S'$ choose a prime $q\notin S'$ larger than 2. Then $\xi=2q$ is a uniformizer at 2, but the component at $q$ has valuation 1. This disproves the displayed inclusion of sets independently of whether any particular kernel vanishes on that set. | issue #2469, item 3 | reviewed |
| R18 | Proposition 14.1.1.1(1), (14.1.1.4), p. 525 | The paper says: “the topological group $W_{E/F}$ is a central extension: $1\to C_E\to W_{E/F}\to\mathrm{Gal}(E/F)\to1$”, for $F$ a global or a local field. This fails: For $\mathbf C/\mathbf R$ the relative Weil group is $\mathbf C^\times\cup j\mathbf C^\times$. Conjugation by $j$ sends $i$ to $-i$, so the kernel $\mathbf C^\times$ is noncentral. | Goyal, *Audit of Masullo's proposed proof*, `dossiers/notes/appendices.md`, “The Artin–Weil product depends on the extension used in its definition”, second paragraph; Naslund, *A proof review of The Music of the Primes*, `review/proof-audit.tex` 361 | reviewed |
| R19 | §14.1.1, decomposition of relative Weil representations, pp. 526–527 | The paper says: A continuous finite-dimensional complex representation of $W_{E_v/F_{v_0}}$ in which every element acts semisimply is a direct sum of one-dimensional representations of this group. This fails: Over $\mathbf Q_2$ the splitting field of $x^3-2$ is the compositum of a totally ramified cubic field and the unramified quadratic field, with Galois group $S_3$. Inflate its irreducible two-dimensional complex representation to the relative Weil group. The image is finite, so every element acts semisimply, but the representation has no one-dimensional summand. Restriction to the abelian subgroup $E_v^\times$ does give a sum of characters. | Goyal, *Audit of Masullo's proposed proof*, `dossiers/notes/appendices.md`, “The Artin–Weil product depends on the extension used in its definition”, second paragraph | reviewed |
| R20 | §14.1.1, inertia invariants and the product (14.1.1.7), p. 527 | The paper says: The pullback of the representation on the inertia invariants $V^0$ is a sum indexed from $1$ to $\dim V$. The local Euler factor uses the same upper limit. This fails: In the two-dimensional irreducible complex representation of $\mathrm{Gal}(\mathbf Q_2(\sqrt[3]2,\zeta_3)/\mathbf Q_2)\cong S_3$, the inertia subgroup $C_3$ fixes only zero. The pullback of $V^0=0$ has no characters and its Euler product is empty. The printed upper limit is $2$. | Goyal, *Audit of Masullo's proposed proof*, `dossiers/notes/appendices.md`, “The Artin–Weil product depends on the extension used in its definition”, second paragraph | reviewed |
| R21 | (14.1.1.8)–(14.1.1.9), p. 527 | The paper says: “$L(s,\rho):=\prod_{v\in\Sigma_E}L_v(s,\rho_v)$”, and for a representation of $W_F$ that factors through $W_{E/F}$: “We define $L(s,\rho):=L(s,\rho_E)$ and observe that this definition is independent of the choice of such $E$”. This fails: The product runs over the places of $E$, with local factors built from quasi-characters of $E_v^\times$ evaluated at a uniformizer of $E_v$. For the trivial one-dimensional representation of $W_{\mathbf Q}$ the choice $E=\mathbf Q$ gives the finite part $\prod_p(1-p^{-s})^{-1}=\zeta(s)$, and $E=\mathbf Q(\sqrt5)$ gives $\prod_v(1-N(v)^{-s})^{-1}=\zeta_{\mathbf Q(\sqrt5)}(s)=\zeta(s)L(s,\chi_5)$; for instance the split prime $11$ contributes $(1-11^{-s})^{-2}$. The two differ (at $s=2$ about $1.645$ against about $1.162$). The product that is stable under inflation runs over the places of $F$, with Frobenius acting on the inertia invariants. | Goyal, *Audit of Masullo's proposed proof*, `report/report.md`, “Other established errors and their reach”, fourth item; `dossiers/notes/appendices.md`, “The Artin–Weil product depends on the extension used in its definition” | reviewed |
| R22 | §14.1.2, pp. 528–529, definition of the central zero | The paper says: $c_0$ is “the unique point of each connected component allowed to occur as a pole of multiplicity higher than 1 of the partial Euler products $\prod_{v\in S}L_v(\chi_v\lvert\cdot\rvert_v^s)$ for any finite set $S$ of places of $F$ containing all the Archimedean places.” This fails: For $F=\mathbf Q(\sqrt5)$ and $\chi=1$ the two real factors give $\Gamma_{\mathbf R}(s)^2$, with double poles at $0,-2,-4,\dots$ for every $S$. The prime $11$ splits ($4^2\equiv5$ mod $11$); with both places above it in $S$ the finite factors are $(1-11^{-s})^{-2}$, with double poles at every $2\pi ik/\log11$, where the real factors are finite and nonzero for $k\ne0$. The bound of Theorem 7.1.3.2(3), on orders exceeding $[F:\mathbf Q]$, is a different statement. | Goyal, *Audit of Masullo's proposed proof*, `dossiers/notes/appendices.md`, “Chapter 14 misidentifies a distinguished pole” | reviewed |
| R23 | §14.1.2, p. 529, and (13.3.1.2), p. 522 | On p. 529 the claimed central point in the fixed chart $s\mapsto\chi\lvert\cdot\rvert_F^s$ has coordinate $1/2-ix_0$. Replacing $\tau$ by $\tau\kappa$ changes $x_0$ by the exponent of $\chi\circ\kappa$, for a continuous homomorphism $\kappa:\mathbf R_{>0}\to C_F^1$. This exponent can be nonzero. Every finite-order character kills the connected image of $\kappa$, so nontriviality on $C_F^1$ alone does not imply a change. For the CM character over $\mathbf Q(i)$ associated with $y^2=x^3-1254^2x$, the straight and rotating complex sections give $x_0=0$ and $x_0=\omega\ne0$. The same $L(s,\chi)$ has the same multiple zero at $1/2$ for both choices. The prescribed coordinate can therefore move while the chart and its divisor stay fixed. Equation (13.3.1.2) evaluates the Mellin jets at $-ix_0$, the corresponding coordinate after subtracting $1/2$. | Goyal, *Audit of Masullo's proposed proof*, `dossiers/notes/trace.md`, “The choice of norm section can expose a multiple central zero”, first and third paragraphs | reviewed |
| R24 | Conjecture 14.1.2.1(2), p. 529 | Conjecture 14.1.2.1(2) allows a multiple zero only at $s=1/2$. The preceding paragraphs call the function “unitarily normalized” and explicitly place its central point at $1/2$, allowing order zero. For the usual $L$-function of a unitary representation, the unitary twist $\pi\otimes\lvert\det\rvert^i$ gives $L(s+i,\pi)$. Applied to the CM curve $y^2=x^3-1254^2x$, it moves a zero of order at least three to $1/2-i$ while preserving the symmetry line. Unitarity alone leaves the imaginary twist undetermined. A convention that re-centres every twisted function would return this multiple zero to $1/2$. The imaginary twist can be fixed by requiring the central character to be trivial on the positive real scalars. The paper should specify the choice in passing from $\pi$ to the normalized function. [Farmer, Pitale, Ryan and Schmidt, §2.2, on balanced automorphic representations](https://arxiv.org/pdf/1711.10375) | Goyal, *Audit of Masullo's proposed proof*, `dossiers/notes/spectral.md`, §8, last paragraph; `dossiers/notes/appendices.md`, “The rank-one case of the proposed Langlands normalization” | reviewed |
| R25 | Conjecture 14.1.2.2, (14.1.2.1), p. 530 | The paper says: For each continuous irreducible finite-dimensional complex representation of $W_{\mathbf Q}$ with semisimple image elements, (14.1.2.1) asserts $L(s,\rho)=L(s,\pi)$ for some cuspidal $\pi$. The automorphic function is completed and unitarily normalized. The text expressly allows Artin–Weil functions without unitary normalization. This fails: In rank one the norm character meets both representation hypotheses. The shift convention on p. 528 gives the completed $\zeta(s+1)$, with a pole at $s=-1$. A completed unitary $\mathrm{GL}_1$ function is entire or has its poles on the lines $\Re s=0,1$. Even an imaginary re-centring on the automorphic side cannot give a pole at $-1$. | Goyal, *Audit of Masullo's proposed proof*, `dossiers/notes/spectral.md`, §8, last paragraph; `dossiers/notes/appendices.md`, “The rank-one case of the proposed Langlands normalization” | reviewed |
| R26 | §14.1.2, p. 533, paragraph beginning “A similar argument as in Proposition 14.1.2.3” | The paper says: “$m_F(\chi)$ will always be larger than the maximum of the dimensions of the irreducible sub-representations of $W_{\mathbf Q}$ occurring as the irreducible constituents of $\mathrm{Ind}_{W_{F'/F}}^{W_{F'/\mathbf Q}}(\chi')$ …, multiplied by the multiplicity with which such irreducible constituent occurs.” This fails: For $F=\mathbf Q(i)$ let $\psi((\alpha))=(\alpha/\bar\alpha)^2$, a Hecke character unramified at every finite place. At a split prime $p=\pi\bar\pi$ the two Euler factors are $(1-ap^{-s})^{-1}$ and $(1-a^{-1}p^{-s})^{-1}$ with $a=(\pi/\bar\pi)^2$, and $a\ne a^{-1}$ because $(\pi)^4\ne(\bar\pi)^4$; at an inert or ramified prime there is a single factor. All poles are simple, so $m_p(\psi)=1$ for every $p$ and $m_F(\psi)=1$. The complex conjugate character is $\psi^{-1}\ne\psi$, so the induction to $W_{\mathbf Q}$ is irreducible of dimension $2$ and occurs once. The claim would give $1\ge2$. | Goyal, *Audit of Masullo's proposed proof*, `report/report.md`, “Other established errors and their reach”, fourth item; `dossiers/notes/trace.md`, “A Gaussian Hecke character disproves the constituent-dimension comparison on p. 533” | reviewed |
| R27 | Remark B.7.2.7, p. 653 | The paper says: For $\chi=\lvert\cdot\rvert_F^{-it_0}$ the reciprocal partial Euler products $E_{f,x}(s,\chi)$ converge to $1/L_f(s,\chi)$ pointwise on $1+i\mathbf R$, and “The convergence over any interval containing $t_0$ in its interior is not uniform.” This fails: For $F=\mathbf Q$ and $t_0=0$ the products $P_X(t)=\prod_{p<X}(1-p^{-1-it})$ converge to $1/\zeta(1+it)$ uniformly on every bounded interval. With $\delta=1/\log X$, comparing the Euler expansions of the logarithms gives $\lvert\log\lvert P_X(t)\rvert-\log\lvert1/\zeta(1+\delta+it)\rvert\rvert\le C$ for all real $t$ and $X\ge3$: the prime powers with exponent at least $2$ contribute a bounded amount, $\sum_{p<X}p^{-1}(1-p^{-\delta})\le\delta\sum_{p<X}(\log p)/p=O(1)$ and $\sum_{p\ge X}p^{-1-\delta}=O(1)$. Since $1/\zeta$ has a simple zero at $1$, $\lvert P_X(t)\rvert\le C'(\lvert t\rvert+1/\log X)$ for $\lvert t\rvert\le1$, and likewise $\lvert1/\zeta(1+it)\rvert\le C'\lvert t\rvert$. On $\lvert t\rvert\le\eta$ the difference is at most $C''(\eta+1/\log X)$, and on $\eta\le\lvert t\rvert\le T$ the convergence is uniform by the remark's own second assertion. Choosing $\eta$ small and then $X$ large gives uniform convergence across $t_0$. | Goyal, *Audit of Masullo's proposed proof*, `dossiers/notes/appendices.md`, “The reciprocal Euler products converge uniformly across the pole ordinate” | reviewed |
| R28 | Example E.2.1.9, weak Hausdorff property, p. 848 | The paper says: The one-point compactification of every Hausdorff space that fails to be locally compact is weakly Hausdorff. This fails: Example E.2.1.10 on the same page gives a counterexample. The Arens–Fort space is Hausdorff and fails to be locally compact at its unique nonisolated point. In its one-point compactification, deleting that point leaves a compact Hausdorff subspace whose image under inclusion is not closed. This violates the weak Hausdorff condition. | Goyal, *Audit of Masullo's proposed proof*, `report/report.md`, “Other established errors and their reach”, fifth item | reviewed |
| R29 | Example E.2.1.9, product of intervals, p. 848 | The paper says: An uncountable product of copies of $[0,1]$ is listed as an example of a Hausdorff space that fails to be locally compact. This fails: The product is compact Hausdorff by Tychonoff’s theorem, so the entire space is a compact neighbourhood of every point. The added point of its one-point compactification is isolated. | Goyal, *Audit of Masullo's proposed proof*, `report/report.md`, “Other established errors and their reach”, fifth item | reviewed |
| R30 | Lemma E.6.3.11, proof after (E.6.3.1), p. 928 | “We have:” $\mathcal X(T')=[\mathcal G(T')\rightrightarrows X(T')]$ (p. 928). The groupoid of points of the quotient stack is identified with the pointwise action groupoid, which omits nontrivial torsors. Take the one-object groupoid with G=ℤ/2, T=* with its trivial torsor x, and T′=S¹→*. These are compactly generated weak Hausdorff spaces. The pointwise action groupoid has one isomorphism class. The trivial double cover and the connected double cover z↦z² are distinct objects up to isomorphism in the stack of torsors on S¹. | Naslund, *A proof review of The Music of the Primes*, `review/proof-audit.tex` 382; `review/page-index.tsv` at 920 | reviewed |

## Cross-references that name the wrong item

Forty places where the text cites an item under the wrong kind or number (a lemma cited as a
proposition, a subsection number given for a display, a reference to a neighbouring item). None
changes the mathematics. They come from the line checks of Eric Naslund's review and were each
compared with the page image.

| Where | What is cited, and what stands there |
|---|---|
| Reference to 1.8.2.2, p. 37 | Page 37 attributes a claim about the growth of pole orders of finite Euler products to “Theorem 1.8.2.2”. Subsection 1.8.2.2 on p. 43 treats theta functions. Display (1.8.2.2) on p. 44 defines a norm and is correctly cited for its parameters elsewhere on p. 37. Neither numbered item states the pole-order claim. |
| Reference to 2.1.3.2, pp. 68, 69, 72, 78, 84, 87, 88, 145, 153, 228, 231 | The text cites “Proposition 2.1.3.2”. The target on p. 68 is Lemma 2.1.3.2. |

| Reference to 2.1.3.2, p. 86 | Page 86 cites “Theorem 2.1.3.2” for the Montel property of the archimedean Schwartz space. The heading on p. 68 is Lemma 2.1.3.2 and treats the global and finite-adelic spaces. Proposition 2.1.4.1(2) on p. 71 supplies the stated property for $S=S_∞$. |
| Reference to 2.1.2.4, pp. 103, 111, 260 | The text cites “Lemma 2.1.2.4”. The target on p. 64 is Proposition 2.1.2.4. |

| Reference to 4.2.1.5, p. 172 | The text cites “Proposition 4.2.1.5”. The target on p. 165 is Lemma 4.2.1.5. |
| Reference to 4.2.2.7, pp. 188, 191 | The text cites “Proposition 4.2.2.7”. The target on p. 173 is display (4.2.2.7). The display appears in Proposition 4.2.2.5. |
| Reference to 2.3.1.2, p. 229 | The text cites “Theorem 2.3.1.2”. The target on p. 100 is Proposition 2.3.1.2. |

Page 232 recalls the meromorphic target described in part (1) beside (5.1.1.1), and its S-local counterpart in part (2). Page 265 displays the S-local operator on the theta quotient, which is (5.1.1.4) in part (3). |
| Reference to 5.2.3.2, pp. 236, 298 | The text cites “Lemma 5.2.3.2”. The target on p. 236 is Proposition 5.2.3.2. |
| Reference to 2.2.3.4, p. 237 | Page 237 cites “Proposition 2.2.3.4” for square integrability of global theta functions. The heading on p. 82 is Theorem 2.2.3.4. Its two-ended rapid decay in part (3) requires both zero terms to vanish. The failure of unrestricted square integrability is recorded separately in I126. |

## Errors of the formalization, not of the paper

Statements or definitions on `main` that were wrong although the paper is right, found by review
or by provers. They are listed because each cost effort and each shows a way Lean can silently
say something other than what was meant.

| Where | What was wrong | Found by |
|---|---|---|
| `tendsto_sum_orthogonalProjections_iff_dense_iSup` (Remark C.3.5.2) | A set ascription outside a supremum made Lean take the union of the subspaces, not their sum. | a prover, #750 |
| `weaklyContDiffOn_iff_scalar` (Definition C.5.1.6) | Stated for any normed source; the paper has k^d, and it fails in infinite dimensions. | a prover, #958 |
| `ElementaryLCAPresentation` ((D.1.4.2)) | The finite factor is `Fin n` with a supplied group structure, but Lean uses addition mod n, so only cyclic factors were covered. Repaired in #2914: the finite factor is a finite type with its own group structure. | provers, #683 and #688 |
| The Z-space quotient maps of §5.1 | Written with the range subtype as codomain, they used the subspace topology, not the transported one. | review of #2258 |
| The eigendistribution weight and complex Gaussian of §3.4 | Inverse weight relative to the action on `main`; a Gaussian whose zeta integral vanishes. | review of #2289 |
| The standard additive character | Followed the paper's signs; see F13. | statement audit |
| The approximation property (Lemma C.2.4.15, §C.3.4): `hilbert_approximationProperty`, the hypothesis of `nuclearTrace`, `dense_finiteRank_iff_forall_targets` | A type ascription to the precompact-convergence synonym did not change the codomain Lean inferred, so density was for bounded convergence (the operator norm): false for ℓ², and too strong as a hypothesis. Repaired in #3330 with a named `HasApproximationProperty`; the review found the same in `dense_finiteRank_iff_forall_sources` and `dense_finiteRank_of_isNuclearTVS`. | provers, #561, #567 and #429 |
| `modulatedCompactSupport_isotypicQuotient_equiv`, `modulatedCompactSupportStage_isotypicQuotient_image` (Theorem 4.5.2.3) | The coverage hypothesis was a section `variable` written with local notations. In the two theorems that include it, it elaborated to `sorry` with no error, so both statements had a hypothesis of type `sorry` and said nothing. Such a `sorry` carries its source position, so the statement hashes moved with every edit to the file and failed the check of every proof there. Repaired in #4014; the signature check now refuses any statement that contains `sorry`, and stopped eight more of the same kind in §7.3 before they were merged. | the merger, and a sweeper independently (#3999) |
| `modulatedCyclicDifference_isClosed_range` (Proposition 4.5.1.1(6)) | The section's hypothesis that the generator has module greater than one (Situation 4.4.2.3) was not included in the theorem, because nothing in its statement mentions it. Without it the statement is false: on ℝ_{>0} × S¹ with the first projection as module, the difference operator of a rotation by a Liouville number does not have closed range. The hypothesis is now included; both uses of the theorem had it at hand. | a sweeper, #3999 |
| `modulatedExponentialDecay_tensor_stage` (Theorem D.3.3.7(1), diagram (D.3.3.4), p. 806) | The claim that the inclusion of a stage into the full decay space is continuous was stated for the wrong topology. The inclusion map is declared with the bare submodule of functions as its target, so `Continuous` took the topology of pointwise convergence, where the paper means the locally convex topology of the decay space; a type ascription on the map did not change that. The two differ already on ℝ_{>0}: translates of a bump to infinity tend to zero pointwise and keep their supremum norm. The statement now names the topology explicitly. | a sweeper, #4414 |
| `WeaklyContDiffOrderOn`, `weakComplexRegularity_iff` (Definition C.5.2.1(2), p. 737) | Each scalar evaluation was required to be continuously differentiable of the given order over the reals. The page says only that it “is an element of $C^k(\Omega)$”, but Theorem C.5.2.6(2) and (C.5.2.6) need complex derivatives: complex conjugation is real-smooth of every order and is not holomorphic. Nothing else used the definition by name, and `weaklyHolomorphicOn_iff_scalar_contDiffOn` already had the complex form. The definition now uses complex derivatives. | wave 1 statement review |
| `IsIntroTraceApproximation` (§1.4.2, pp. 28–29) | The cutoffs and the mollifiers were required to be `ContDiff ℝ ⊤`. In the current Mathlib the order `⊤` is the analytic one, and a real-analytic function of compact support is zero, so nothing satisfied the definition: every theorem that assumes an approximation was vacuous, and the statement that one exists was false. The page has test functions in $\mathcal D(\mathbf R_{>0})$ and a smooth mollifier. The orders are now `(⊤ : ℕ∞)`, and `exists_introTraceApproximation` is proved. No other statement of the library has this slip. Repaired in #6450. | FleckiBunti, #6425; existence proof by sflicht; pull request by corneliusroemer-agent |

## How this file is kept

Digesters and provers mark a problem where they meet it with a comment `-- TODO(paper-gap): …`
in the Lean file and describe it in their report. When the pull request is merged, the integrator
reads the passage and adds an entry here. `grep -rn "TODO(paper-gap)" RiemannMasullo` lists every
mark in the code.

Contributors report what they find on the issue: a counterexample, a mismatch between the Lean
and the print, or a step of a printed proof that fails. The integrator checks each report against
the page and decides what becomes of the statement ([`AGENTS.md`](AGENTS.md), "False statements
and gaps").

A statement that is false as printed is also kept in Lean, in the section's `_Printed.lean`
file: the printed claim as a proposition `Printed.<name>`, and a theorem `Printed.<name>_false`
that proves its negation. Where that proof is finished and Lean reports that it rests on nothing
unproved (`#print axioms` shows no `sorryAx`), the entry's **Checked** column says *Lean*: such an
entry rests on the proof checker, not on a reading. A refutation proved from theorems of the
library that are themselves still unproved is named in the entry as such and does not change the
column; it becomes a checked refutation when those theorems are proved. Until 22 November this
distinction was not made, and forty entries were marked *Lean* too early; they have been set
back to what they were. What the proof checker establishes is that the proposition, as written
in Lean, is false. Whether the proof uses the reason the entry gives is compared separately,
entry by entry, and the entry says so where a refutation depends on a reading of the notation
or on a boundary case. F2 is the first
(`RiemannMasullo/AppA_AlgebraicNT/Sec1_Printed.lean`); the entries made before this rule are
being brought into this form, and each row names its proposition once it exists.

A statement that can be neither proved nor refuted is recorded as a gap: a named theorem left
unproved, with a `Gap:` line in its docstring and an issue labelled `gap`. Gaps are listed under
"Placeholders and missing material".
